		.Case: Sarah, a 42-year-old software developer, presents with a 3-month history of neck pain and headaches. She reports that her symptoms began gradually and have worsened over time. Sarah describes a constant dull ache (3/10) in her upper neck that radiates to her right temple. The pain intensifies to 7/10 during headache episodes, which occur 2-3 times per week. She notes that her symptoms are exacerbated by prolonged computer use and improve with rest and gentle neck movements. Sarah has tried over-the-counter pain medications with minimal relief.
			(Γ)
				Classification
					.✓42-year-old → <50 = Mobility deficits, no Coordination classification >50 
					.✓3-month history of neck pain and headaches → at exact 12 weeks = could be Mobility deficits or Coordination classification
				HA
					.constant dull ache → ✗Stenosis carotid artery
					.ache in her upper neck that radiates to her right temple → ✗Radiating = Radiculopaty? // unilateral = ✗stenosis, ✓cervicogenic // ✓from neck to head = Cervicogenic
					.headache episodes, which occur 2-3 times per week → Goes and comes = ✗migraine, ✓cervicogenic
					.symptoms are exacerbated by prolonged computer use and improve with rest and gentle neck movements → ✓Cervicogenic HA, ✓Neck Pain with Headache (¦Oso)
					.over-the-counter pain medications with minimal relief → ✗Stenosis carotid artery

			Q1.Based on the information provided, which classification category does Sarah's condition most likely fall under?
				-Answer: Neck Pain with Cervicogenic Headache.
				-Rationale: The case description aligns with the common symptoms of Neck Pain with Cervicogenic Headache as outlined in the Clinical Practice Guidelines. Specifically, Sarah presents with "non-continuous, unilateral neck pain and associated (referred) headache" that is "precipitated or aggravated by neck movements or sustained positions/postures" (computer use in this case).

			Q2.What are the expected examination findings for this classification?
				-Answer: Expected examination findings for Neck Pain with Cervicogenic Headache include:
					Decreased upper cervical spine mobility
					Headache reproduced with provocation of involved upper cervical segments
					Limited cervical range of motion
					Restricted upper cervical segmental mobility
					Strength, endurance, and coordination deficits of neck muscles
				-Rationale: These findings are directly listed in the Clinical Practice Guidelines for the Neck Pain with Headache (Cervicogenic) classification.

			Q3.What are the recommended interventions for this classification?
				Answer: Recommended interventions for Neck Pain with Cervicogenic Headache include:
					1. Coordination exercises
					2. Strengthening exercises
					3. Endurance exercises
					4. Stretching exercises
					5. Upper cervical mobility exercises/mobilization
				Rationale: These interventions are specifically listed in the "Recommended Interventions" section for Neck Pain with Headache (Cervicogenic) in the provided Clinical Practice Guidelines summary.

			Q4.What additional tests or assessments might be beneficial to confirm this classification and guide treatment?
				Answer: Additional tests or assessments that might be beneficial include:
					1. Cranial cervical flexion test
					2. Deep flexor endurance test
					3. Cervical rotation/flexion test for C1-2 mobility
					4. Assessment of cervical range of motion
					5. Segmental mobility assessment of the upper cervical spine (C0-C2)

			Rationale: These tests are mentioned in the "Neck Pain with Headache" section of the classification descriptions. They help assess the specific impairments associated with cervicogenic headaches, such as restricted upper cervical mobility, impaired control of deep neck flexors, and limited cervical range of motion.

		.Case: Emily, a 28-year-old graphic designer, presents with a 6-week history of neck pain following a minor car accident. She reports constant, dull pain (4/10) in her mid to lower cervical spine that occasionally radiates to her left shoulder. Emily experiences intermittent dizziness, especially when turning her head quickly. She also complains of difficulty concentrating at work and increased sensitivity to bright computer screens. Her symptoms worsen after long periods of computer use and improve with rest. Emily has been taking over-the-counter pain medications with minimal relief.

			Q1. Based on the information provided, which classification category does Emily's condition most likely fall under?

			Answer: Emily's condition most likely falls under the classification of Neck Pain with Movement Coordination Impairments (Whiplash Associated Disorders WAD).

			Rationale: The case description aligns with several key symptoms of WAD as outlined in the Clinical Practice Guidelines. Specifically, Emily presents with a mechanism of onset linked to trauma (car accident), associated shoulder pain, dizziness, difficulty with concentration, and hypersensitivity to light stimuli (bright computer screens).

			Q2. What are the expected examination findings for this classification?

			Answer: Expected examination findings for Neck Pain with Movement Coordination Impairments include:
			Strength and endurance deficits of the neck muscles
			Decreased neck flexor muscle endurance
			Neck pain with mid-range motion that worsens with end-range positions
			Point tenderness and myofascial trigger points
			Altered muscle activation, proprioceptive deficits, or postural control
			Neck and referred pain reproduced by provocation of involved cervical segments

			Rationale: These findings are directly listed in the Clinical Practice Guidelines for the Neck Pain with Movement Coordination Impairments classification.

			Q3. What are the recommended interventions for this classification?

			Answer: Recommended interventions for Neck Pain with Movement Coordination Impairments include:
			1. Coordination exercises
			2. Strengthening exercises
			3. Endurance exercises
			4. Stretching exercises
			5. Education on good prognosis and advice to stay active

			Rationale: These interventions are specifically listed in the "Recommended Interventions" section for Neck Pain with Movement Coordination Impairment Whiplash Associated Disorders (WAD) in the provided Clinical Practice Guidelines summary.

			Q4. What additional tests or assessments might be beneficial to confirm this classification and guide treatment?

			Answer: Additional tests or assessments that might be beneficial include:
			1. Cranial cervical flexion test
			2. Deep flexor endurance test
			3. Assessment of cervical range of motion
			4. Evaluation of postural control and proprioception
			5. Screening for vestibular dysfunction due to reported dizziness
			6. Assessment of upper quarter muscle flexibility and strength

			Rationale: These tests are relevant to the "Expected exam findings" for Neck Pain with Movement Coordination Impairments. They help assess the specific impairments associated with WAD, such as decreased neck flexor endurance, altered muscle activation, proprioceptive deficits, and postural control issues. The vestibular screening is added due to Emily's reported dizziness.

		.Case: Mark, a 45-year-old construction worker, presents with a 3-month history of neck pain and right arm symptoms following a fall from a ladder at work. He reports constant, moderate pain (5/10) in his right lower cervical region that radiates down his right arm to his thumb and index finger. Mark experiences intermittent numbness and tingling in the same distribution. He notes that his symptoms worsen with looking up or turning his head to the right, and when using his right arm for overhead activities. Mark has difficulty sleeping due to pain and has been taking over-the-counter pain medications with minimal relief.

			Q1. Based on the information provided, which classification category does Mark's condition most likely fall under?

			Answer: Mark's condition most likely falls under the classification of Neck Pain with Radiating Pain (Radicular).

			Rationale: The case description aligns with the common symptoms of Neck Pain with Radiating Pain as outlined in the Clinical Practice Guidelines. Specifically, Mark presents with "neck pain with radiating pain into upper extremity on involved side" and "diminished UE sensation", which are key features of this classification.

			Q2. What are the expected examination findings for this classification?

			Answer: Expected examination findings for Neck Pain with Radiating Pain include:
			Radiating pain reproduced or relieved with radiculopathy testing
			Decreased ipsilateral range of motion
			Relief with cervical distraction
			Positive Upper Limb Tension Test (ULTT) for Median Nerve
			Positive Spurling's Test
			May have upper extremity sensory, strength, or reflex deficits associated with the involved nerve roots

			Rationale: These findings are directly listed in the Clinical Practice Guidelines for the Neck Pain with Radiating Pain classification.

			Q3. What are the recommended interventions for this classification?

			Answer: Recommended interventions for Neck Pain with Radiating Pain include:
			1. Cervical traction/distraction
			2. Coordination exercises
			3. Strengthening exercises
			4. Endurance exercises
			5. Stretching exercises
			6. Education on good prognosis and advice to stay active

			Rationale: These interventions are specifically listed in the "Recommended Interventions" section for Neck Pain with Radiating Pain in the provided Clinical Practice Guidelines summary.

			Q4. What additional tests or assessments might be beneficial to confirm this classification and guide treatment?

			Answer: Additional tests or assessments that might be beneficial include:
			1. Cervical range of motion assessment, particularly ipsilateral rotation and extension
			2. Upper Limb Tension Test (ULTT) for the median nerve
			3. Spurling's Test
			4. Cervical distraction test
			5. Neurological examination including myotome strength testing, dermatomal sensory testing, and deep tendon reflex testing for C5-T1
			6. Cervical segmental mobility assessment

			Rationale: These tests are mentioned in the "Expected exam findings" for Neck Pain with Radiating Pain. They help assess the specific impairments associated with cervical radiculopathy, such as nerve root compression, altered neurodynamics, and segmental mobility restrictions. The neurological examination is crucial to determine the specific nerve root(s) involved and the extent of neurological deficit.

		.Case: Lisa, a 35-year-old yoga instructor, presents with a 2-month history of intermittent neck pain and stiffness. She reports a gradual onset of symptoms without any specific injury. Lisa describes a constant, dull ache (3/10) in her mid-cervical spine that occasionally radiates to both shoulders. She experiences increased pain and stiffness in the morning, which improves somewhat with gentle movement. Lisa notes that certain yoga poses, particularly those involving neck extension or rotation, exacerbate her symptoms. She has been using over-the-counter anti-inflammatory medications with minimal relief.

			Q1. Based on the information provided, which classification category does Lisa's condition most likely fall under?

			Answer: Lisa's condition most likely falls under the classification of Neck Pain with Mobility Deficits.

			Rationale: The case description aligns with the common symptoms of Neck Pain with Mobility Deficits as outlined in the Clinical Practice Guidelines. Specifically, Lisa presents with "central and/or unilateral neck pain" and "limitation in neck motion that consistently reproduces symptoms" (certain yoga poses exacerbating symptoms). The gradual onset and age (<50 years) also support this classification.

			Q2. What are the expected examination findings for this classification?

			Answer: Expected examination findings for Neck Pain with Mobility Deficits include:
			Limited cervical range of motion (ROM)
			Neck pain at end ranges of active/passive ROM
			Restricted cervical and thoracic segmental mobility
			Neck and referred pain reproduced with provocation of the involved cervical or upper thoracic segments
			Possible deficits in cervico-scapula-thoracic strength and motor control

			Rationale: These findings are directly listed in the Clinical Practice Guidelines for the Neck Pain with Mobility Deficits classification.

			Q3. What are the recommended interventions for this classification?

			Answer: Recommended interventions for Neck Pain with Mobility Deficits include:
			1. Manipulation/Mobilization of Cervical Spine/Thoracic Spine
			2. Range of Motion exercises
			3. Coordination exercises
			4. Strengthening exercises
			5. Endurance exercises
			6. Stretching exercises
			7. General Fitness Training
			8. Advice to Stay Active

			Rationale: These interventions are specifically listed in the "Recommended Interventions" section for Neck Pain with Mobility Deficits in the provided Clinical Practice Guidelines summary.

			Q4. What additional tests or assessments might be beneficial to confirm this classification and guide treatment?

			Answer: Additional tests or assessments that might be beneficial include:
			1. Detailed cervical active and passive range of motion assessment
			2. Segmental mobility assessment of the cervical and upper thoracic spine
			3. Cervico-scapula-thoracic muscle strength and endurance testing
			4. Postural assessment, particularly during yoga poses that exacerbate symptoms
			5. Palpation for areas of tenderness or muscle guarding
			6. Neurological screening to rule out radicular involvement

			Rationale: These tests align with the "Expected exam findings" for Neck Pain with Mobility Deficits. They help assess the specific impairments associated with this classification, such as limited ROM, restricted segmental mobility, and potential muscle strength/endurance deficits. The postural assessment during yoga poses is added to address Lisa's specific functional limitations.

		.Case: David, a 52-year-old accountant, presents with a 4-month history of chronic neck pain and associated headaches. He reports a gradual onset of symptoms without any specific injury. David describes a constant, dull ache (4/10) in his upper cervical spine that frequently radiates to his occipital region and forehead, primarily on the left side. He experiences headaches 3-4 times a week, which are aggravated by prolonged desk work and alleviated somewhat by changing positions or gentle neck movements. David notes increased difficulty with concentration during headache episodes and occasional dizziness when looking up or turning his head quickly. He has been taking over-the-counter pain medications with minimal relief.

			Q1. Based on the information provided, which classification category does David's condition most likely fall under?

			Answer: David's condition most likely falls under the classification of Neck Pain with Cervicogenic Headache.

			Rationale: The case description aligns with the common symptoms of Neck Pain with Cervicogenic Headache as outlined in the Clinical Practice Guidelines. Specifically, David presents with "non-continuous, unilateral neck pain and associated (referred) headache" that is "precipitated or aggravated by neck movements or sustained positions/postures" (prolonged desk work in this case).

			Q2. What are the expected examination findings for this classification?

			Answer: Expected examination findings for Neck Pain with Cervicogenic Headache include:
			Decreased upper cervical spine mobility
			Headache reproduced with provocation of involved upper cervical segments
			Limited cervical range of motion
			Restricted upper cervical segmental mobility
			Strength, endurance, and coordination deficits of neck muscles

			Rationale: These findings are directly listed in the Clinical Practice Guidelines for the Neck Pain with Headache (Cervicogenic) classification.

			Q3. What are the recommended interventions for this classification?

			Answer: Recommended interventions for Neck Pain with Cervicogenic Headache include:
			1. Coordination exercises
			2. Strengthening exercises
			3. Endurance exercises
			4. Stretching exercises
			5. Upper cervical mobility exercises/mobilization

			Rationale: These interventions are specifically listed in the "Recommended Interventions" section for Neck Pain with Headache (Cervicogenic) in the provided Clinical Practice Guidelines summary.

			Q4. What additional tests or assessments might be beneficial to confirm this classification and guide treatment?

			Answer: Additional tests or assessments that might be beneficial include:
			1. Cervical rotation/flexion test for C1-2 mobility
			2. Cranial cervical flexion test to assess deep neck flexor control
			3. Deep flexor endurance test
			4. Assessment of cervical range of motion, particularly upper cervical
			5. Segmental mobility assessment of the upper cervical spine (C0-C2)
			6. Palpation and provocation of upper cervical segments to reproduce headache
			7. Vestibular screening due to reported dizziness

			Rationale: These tests are mentioned in the "Neck Pain with Headache" section of the classification descriptions. They help assess the specific impairments associated with cervicogenic headaches, such as restricted upper cervical mobility, impaired control of deep neck flexors, and limited cervical range of motion. The vestibular screening is added due to David's reported dizziness with certain head movements.

		.Case: Emma, a 29-year-old professional dancer, presents with a 6-week history of neck pain following a minor fall during rehearsal. She reports intermittent, sharp pain (6/10) in her left lower cervical region that occasionally radiates down her left arm to her thumb and index finger. Emma experiences numbness and tingling in her left hand, particularly after long rehearsals or performances. She notes difficulty with certain dance moves, especially those requiring full cervical rotation or arm elevation. Emma has been taking over-the-counter pain medications and using ice with minimal relief.

			Q1. Based on the information provided, which classification category does Emma's condition most likely fall under?

			Answer: Emma's condition most likely falls under the classification of Neck Pain with Radiating Pain (Radicular).

			Rationale: The case description aligns with the common symptoms of Neck Pain with Radiating Pain as outlined in the Clinical Practice Guidelines. Specifically, Emma presents with "neck pain with radiating pain into upper extremity on involved side" and "diminished UE sensation", which are key features of this classification. The mechanism of injury (fall) and specific pattern of symptoms (radiating to thumb and index finger) suggest possible C6 or C7 nerve root involvement.

			Q2. What are the expected examination findings for this classification?

			Answer: Expected examination findings for Neck Pain with Radiating Pain include:
			Radiating pain reproduced or relieved with radiculopathy testing
			Decreased ipsilateral range of motion
			Relief with cervical distraction
			Positive Upper Limb Tension Test (ULTT) for Median Nerve
			Positive Spurling's Test
			May have upper extremity sensory, strength, or reflex deficits associated with the involved nerve roots

			Rationale: These findings are directly listed in the Clinical Practice Guidelines for the Neck Pain with Radiating Pain classification.

			Q3. What are the recommended interventions for this classification?

			Answer: Recommended interventions for Neck Pain with Radiating Pain include:
			1. Cervical traction/distraction
			2. Coordination exercises
			3. Strengthening exercises
			4. Endurance exercises
			5. Stretching exercises
			6. Education on good prognosis and advice to stay active

			Rationale: These interventions are specifically listed in the "Recommended Interventions" section for Neck Pain with Radiating Pain in the provided Clinical Practice Guidelines summary.

			Q4. What additional tests or assessments might be beneficial to confirm this classification and guide treatment?

			Answer: Additional tests or assessments that might be beneficial include:
			1. Cervical range of motion assessment, particularly left rotation and extension
			2. Upper Limb Tension Test (ULTT) for the median nerve
			3. Spurling's Test
			4. Cervical distraction test
			5. Neurological examination including myotome strength testing, dermatomal sensory testing, and deep tendon reflex testing for C5-T1
			6. Cervical segmental mobility assessment
			7. Scapular stability and control assessment
			8. Functional movement assessment specific to dance movements that provoke symptoms

			Rationale: These tests are mentioned in the "Expected exam findings" for Neck Pain with Radiating Pain. They help assess the specific impairments associated with cervical radiculopathy, such as nerve root compression, altered neurodynamics, and segmental mobility restrictions. The neurological examination is crucial to determine the specific nerve root(s) involved and the extent of neurological deficit. The additional functional movement assessment is included due to Emma's specific needs as a dancer.

		.Case: Margaret, a 68-year-old retired teacher, presents with a 3-week history of sudden-onset, severe headaches. She describes a constant, throbbing pain (8/10) localized to the left side of her head, particularly intense around her left temple and behind her left eye. Margaret reports associated symptoms of intermittent blurred vision in her left eye, occasional dizziness, and difficulty finding words during conversations. She notes that the pain worsens when she lies flat and improves slightly when sitting upright. Margaret has a history of hypertension and smoking. Over-the-counter pain medications provide minimal relief.

			Q1. Based on the information provided, which classification category does Margaret's condition most likely fall under?

			Answer: Margaret's condition most likely falls under the category of headache caused by stenosis of the carotid artery, rather than the typical neck pain classifications outlined in the Clinical Practice Guidelines.

			Rationale: The case description aligns closely with the symptoms of carotid artery stenosis as mentioned in the search results for "Headache 1". Specifically, Margaret presents with:
			Constant, throbbing pain localized to one side of the head
			Sudden onset of severe headaches
			Associated neurological symptoms (blurred vision, difficulty finding words)
			Minimal relief with pain medications
			Symptoms affected by position (worse when lying flat)
			Presence of vascular risk factors (hypertension, history of smoking)

			Q2. What are the expected examination findings for this condition?

			Answer: While the Clinical Practice Guidelines don't specifically outline examination findings for carotid artery stenosis, based on the nature of the condition, we might expect:
			Possible carotid bruit on auscultation
			Potentially decreased pulse in the affected carotid artery
			Normal or limited cervical range of motion without reproduction of primary symptoms
			Possible signs of transient ischemic attacks during examination

			Q3. What are the recommended interventions for this condition?

			Answer: The primary recommendation for suspected carotid artery stenosis is urgent medical referral. Physical therapy interventions are not appropriate for this condition.

			Rationale: As stated in the search results, for a headache caused by stenosis of the carotid artery, the recommendation is "Definitely refer probably urgent".

			Q4. What additional tests or assessments might be beneficial to confirm this classification and guide treatment?

			Answer: As a physical therapist, the most important action is to recognize the red flags and refer for immediate medical evaluation. Additional medical tests that might be performed by a physician include:
			1. Carotid duplex ultrasonography
			2. Magnetic Resonance Angiography (MRA)
			3. Computed Tomography Angiography (CTA)
			4. Neurological examination
			5. Blood tests to assess for cardiovascular risk factors

			Rationale: These tests are beyond the scope of physical therapy practice but are crucial for diagnosing and managing carotid artery stenosis. The physical therapist's role is to recognize the potential seriousness of the condition and facilitate urgent medical care.

			This case emphasizes the importance of recognizing symptoms that may indicate serious pathology beyond the typical musculoskeletal conditions addressed in physical therapy. It highlights the need for immediate referral when encountering potential vascular causes of head and neck pain.
