		This list groups signs and symptoms with their associated neck and thoracic conditions, providing a comprehensive reference for clinicians to identify and differentiate between various conditions based on clinical presentation.

		---------------------------------------------------------------------------------

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Dizziness or Vertigo: When the head is placed in certain positions, dizziness or vertigo may occur due to reduced blood flow.
		- Nystagmus: Involuntary eye movements can indicate vestibular or central nervous system involvement.
		- Nausea: Feeling of sickness or discomfort in the stomach.
		- Tinnitus: Ringing or buzzing in the ears.
		- Visual Disturbances: Such as diplopia (double vision) or blurred vision.
		- Drop Attacks: Sudden falls without loss of consciousness.
		- Dysarthria: Difficulty in articulating words.
		- Dysphagia: Difficulty swallowing.
		- Ataxia: Lack of voluntary coordination of muscle movements.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Hyperreflexia: Exaggerated reflexes indicating spinal cord involvement.
		- Hoffman's Sign: A reflex test where flicking the distal phalanx of the middle finger causes thumb flexion, suggesting upper motor neuron lesion.
		- Clonus: Involuntary muscle contractions indicating central nervous system involvement.
		- Babinski Sign: Upward movement of the big toe when the sole of the foot is stimulated, indicating upper motor neuron lesion.
		- Gait Disturbances: Difficulty walking or maintaining balance due to spinal cord compression.
		- Bilateral Neurological Signs: Symptoms affecting both sides of the body, such as numbness or weakness, indicating central cord involvement.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Restricted Movement: Limited ability to move the neck in one or more directions.
		- Pain with Movement: Discomfort or pain during neck movements, potentially indicating nerve involvement.
		- Neurological Symptoms: Tingling, numbness, or weakness during ROM testing, suggesting spinal cord or nerve root compression.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Hoffman's Sign: Indicates upper motor neuron lesion if positive.
		- Babinski Sign: Positive if the big toe extends upward, indicating a central nervous system issue.
		- Clonus: Repeated muscle contractions suggest central nervous system involvement.
		- Spasticity: Increased muscle tone or stiffness, indicating upper motor neuron lesion.
		- Weakness: Muscle weakness, particularly if bilateral, suggesting spinal cord compression.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Reproduction of Symptoms: Pain, tingling, or numbness in the arm that replicates the patient's symptoms.
		- Limited Range of Motion: Inability to fully extend the elbow or wrist due to pain or discomfort.
		- Increased Symptoms with Contralateral Cervical Flexion: Worsening of symptoms when the neck is bent away from the tested side.
		- Relief with Ipsilateral Cervical Flexion: Reduction of symptoms when the neck is bent towards the tested side.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Reproduction of Radicular Pain: Pain radiating down the arm that mimics the patient's usual symptoms.
		- Increased Neck Pain: Localized pain in the neck that may accompany radicular symptoms.
		- Neurological Symptoms: Tingling, numbness, or weakness in the arm or hand.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Symptom Relief: Reduction or elimination of arm pain or tingling when the hand is placed on the head.
		- Decreased Neurological Symptoms: Reduction in numbness or weakness in the arm.
		- Improved Range of Motion: Increased ability to move the neck or arm without discomfort.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Abnormal Eye Movements: Nystagmus or other involuntary eye movements could indicate central nervous system involvement.
		- Hearing Loss: Unilateral hearing loss may suggest a central cause if not associated with BPPV.
		- Facial Weakness: Weakness or asymmetry in facial muscles could indicate a cranial nerve lesion.
		- Dysphagia or Dysarthria: Difficulty swallowing or speaking may point to brainstem involvement.
		- Ataxia: Lack of coordination or balance issues could suggest cerebellar involvement.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Reduction of Symptoms: Relief of headache or neck pain when the test is performed.
		- Palpable Clunk: A noticeable clunk during the test, indicating movement of the atlas on the axis.
		- Feeling of Instability: The patient may report a sensation of instability or shifting in the neck.
		- Neurological Symptoms: Presence of symptoms such as dizziness or tingling that improve with the test.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Excessive Movement: Greater than expected movement during the test, suggesting ligament laxity.
		- Delayed Movement of C2: A delay in the movement of the axis when the head is side-bent or rotated.
		- Reproduction of Symptoms: Provocation of headache or neck pain during the test.
		- Neurological Symptoms: Symptoms such as dizziness or visual disturbances that occur during the test.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Tenderness: Pain upon palpation of specific muscles or regions.
		- Referred Pain: Pain that radiates to the head or other areas when a trigger point is palpated.
		- Muscle Tightness: Increased muscle tone or resistance to palpation.
		- Limited Range of Motion: Restricted movement due to muscle tension.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Pain Relief: Reduction in headache or neck pain following manual therapy.
		- Increased Range of Motion: Improved cervical mobility after treatment.
		- Decreased Muscle Tension: Reduction in muscle tightness and tenderness.
		- Improved Function: Enhanced ability to perform daily activities without pain.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Restricted Movement: Limited ability to move the neck in one or more directions.
		- Pain with Movement: Discomfort or pain during neck movements, indicating possible joint or soft tissue restrictions.
		- Asymmetry: Unequal movement between the left and right sides of the neck.
		- End-Range Pain: Pain occurring at the end of the available range of motion, suggesting joint or soft tissue involvement.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Increased Range of Motion: Improvement in cervical mobility following manual therapy.
		- Pain Relief: Reduction in neck pain after treatment.
		- Decreased Muscle Tension: Reduction in muscle tightness and tenderness.
		- Improved Function: Enhanced ability to perform daily activities without pain.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Muscle Weakness: Inability to generate sufficient force during isometric or dynamic testing.
		- Poor Coordination: Difficulty performing smooth and controlled movements, indicating neuromuscular control issues.
		- Fatigue: Rapid onset of muscle fatigue during endurance testing, suggesting poor muscle endurance.
		- Postural Instability: Inability to maintain proper posture during testing, indicating deficits in muscle control and coordination.
		- Pain During Testing: Reproduction of neck pain during muscle performance tasks, suggesting involvement of specific muscle groups in the patient's symptoms.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Tenderness: Pain upon palpation of specific muscles or joints in the cervical region.
		- Referred Pain: Pain that radiates to the head or other areas when a trigger point is palpated.
		- Muscle Tightness: Increased muscle tone or resistance to palpation, indicating tension.
		- Joint Stiffness: Reduced mobility or stiffness in the cervical joints upon palpation.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Reproduction of Symptoms: Pain, tingling, or numbness in the arm that replicates the patient's symptoms.
		- Limited Range of Motion: Inability to fully extend the elbow or wrist due to pain or discomfort.
		- Increased Symptoms with Contralateral Cervical Flexion: Worsening of symptoms when the neck is bent away from the tested side.
		- Relief with Ipsilateral Cervical Flexion: Reduction of symptoms when the neck is bent towards the tested side.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Reproduction of Radicular Pain: Pain radiating down the arm that mimics the patient's usual symptoms.
		- Increased Neck Pain: Localized pain in the neck that may accompany radicular symptoms.
		- Neurological Symptoms: Tingling, numbness, or weakness in the arm or hand.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Decreased or Absent Pulse: A reduction or loss of the radial pulse during Adson’s test suggests vascular compression.
		- Pain or Heaviness: Discomfort or a feeling of heaviness in the arm during Roos test indicates possible vascular involvement.
		- Numbness or Tingling: Sensory changes in the arm or hand during testing suggest compromised blood flow.
		- Color Changes: Pallor or cyanosis in the hand during testing may indicate vascular insufficiency.
		- Swelling: Edema in the arm or hand, potentially due to venous compression.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Numbness or Tingling: Sensory changes in the arm or hand during testing suggest nerve compression.
		- Weakness: Reduced strength in the hand or arm, potentially due to nerve involvement.
		- Pain: Discomfort or aching in the neck, shoulder, or arm during testing.
		- Reproduction of Symptoms: Provocation of the patient's usual symptoms during the test.
		- Decreased or Absent Pulse: Although more indicative of vascular TOS, a change in pulse during Adson’s test can suggest neurovascular involvement.

		Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
		- Reproduction of Symptoms: Pain, tingling, or numbness in the arm that replicates the patient's symptoms.
		- Limited Range of Motion: Inability to fully extend the elbow or wrist due to pain or discomfort.
		- Increased Symptoms with Contralateral Cervical Flexion: Worsening of symptoms when the neck is bent away from the tested side.
		- Relief with Ipsilateral Cervical Flexion: Reduction of symptoms when the neck is bent towards the tested side.
	↑----------------------------------------------------------------------

	I) Cervical Artery Disease (CAD)

	Tests and Measures

	- T&M1a. Vertebral Artery Testing: This test is specifically designed to assess the integrity of the vertebral arteries, which supply blood to the posterior part of the brain. It is crucial for identifying potential cervical artery disease, which can lead to serious complications such as stroke if not properly managed.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: The vertebral artery test is used to identify any compromise in blood flow through the vertebral arteries, which can be caused by mechanical compression or other vascular issues. This is important because reduced blood flow can lead to symptoms such as dizziness, vertigo, and even loss of consciousness.
	  - Procedure: The test involves positioning the patient's head in extension and rotation to one side, which can compress the contralateral vertebral artery. The clinician observes for symptoms such as dizziness, nystagmus, or other neurological signs that indicate compromised blood flow.
	  - Clinical Importance: Identifying vertebral artery insufficiency is critical before performing any cervical manipulations or exercises that could exacerbate the condition. It helps prevent potentially serious complications by ensuring that any interventions are safe for the patient.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Dizziness or Vertigo: When the head is placed in certain positions, dizziness or vertigo may occur due to reduced blood flow.
	  - Nystagmus: Involuntary eye movements can indicate vestibular or central nervous system involvement.
	  - Nausea: Feeling of sickness or discomfort in the stomach.
	  - Tinnitus: Ringing or buzzing in the ears.
	  - Visual Disturbances: Such as diplopia (double vision) or blurred vision.
	  - Drop Attacks: Sudden falls without loss of consciousness.
	  - Dysarthria: Difficulty in articulating words.
	  - Dysphagia: Difficulty swallowing.
	  - Ataxia: Lack of voluntary coordination of muscle movements.

	These symptoms suggest that the vertebral artery is not adequately supplying blood to the brain, potentially due to mechanical compression or other vascular issues. Identifying these signs is crucial for preventing serious complications and ensuring safe treatment for patients with cervical artery disease.

	--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

	II) Cervical Myelopathy

	Tests and Measures

	- T&M2. UQS (Upper Quarter Screen): This screening tool includes upper motor neuron testing, which is relevant for identifying cervical myelopathy.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: The Upper Quarter Screen (UQS) is designed to assess both sensory and motor function in the upper extremities. For cervical myelopathy, the focus is on identifying signs of upper motor neuron involvement, such as hyperreflexia, muscle weakness, and altered sensation.
	  - Procedure: The UQS involves testing deep tendon reflexes, myotomes, dermatomes, and specific upper motor neuron signs like Hoffman's sign. These tests help identify neurological deficits that suggest spinal cord compression.
	  - Clinical Importance: Early detection of cervical myelopathy is crucial to prevent progression of spinal cord damage. The UQS helps clinicians determine the need for further diagnostic imaging and potential surgical intervention.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Hyperreflexia: Exaggerated reflexes indicating spinal cord involvement.
	  - Hoffman's Sign: A reflex test where flicking the distal phalanx of the middle finger causes thumb flexion, suggesting upper motor neuron lesion.
	  - Clonus: Involuntary muscle contractions indicating central nervous system involvement.
	  - Babinski Sign: Upward movement of the big toe when the sole of the foot is stimulated, indicating upper motor neuron lesion.
	  - Gait Disturbances: Difficulty walking or maintaining balance due to spinal cord compression.
	  - Bilateral Neurological Signs: Symptoms affecting both sides of the body, such as numbness or weakness, indicating central cord involvement.

	- T&M4. Cervical ROM Assessment (AROM and PROM): Helps identify limitations or abnormalities in movement that might suggest myelopathy.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: Assessing active and passive range of motion (AROM and PROM) in the cervical spine helps identify mechanical restrictions or neurological deficits.
	  - Procedure: The clinician evaluates the patient's ability to move their neck in all directions (flexion, extension, rotation, lateral flexion) and notes any limitations or pain.
	  - Clinical Importance: Restricted ROM can indicate underlying pathology such as myelopathy, where neurological deficits may limit movement. It also helps differentiate between mechanical and neurological causes of neck pain.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Restricted Movement: Limited ability to move the neck in one or more directions.
	  - Pain with Movement: Discomfort or pain during neck movements, potentially indicating nerve involvement.
	  - Neurological Symptoms: Tingling, numbness, or weakness during ROM testing, suggesting spinal cord or nerve root compression.

	- T&M10. Special Tests: Specific tests like Hoffman's sign or Babinski can indicate upper motor neuron involvement.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: Special tests are used to identify specific neurological signs that suggest upper motor neuron lesions, which are characteristic of cervical myelopathy.
	  - Procedure: Tests such as Hoffman's sign, Babinski's sign, and clonus are performed to assess the presence of upper motor neuron involvement.
	  - Clinical Importance: Positive results on these tests indicate potential spinal cord compression and warrant further investigation, such as MRI, to assess the extent of myelopathy.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Hoffman's Sign: Indicates upper motor neuron lesion if positive.
	  - Babinski Sign: Positive if the big toe extends upward, indicating a central nervous system issue.
	  - Clonus: Repeated muscle contractions suggest central nervous system involvement.
	  - Spasticity: Increased muscle tone or stiffness, indicating upper motor neuron lesion.
	  - Weakness: Muscle weakness, particularly if bilateral, suggesting spinal cord compression.

	Conditions and Tests & Measures Not Used

	- Conditions Not Addressed: Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV), Vascular TOS, Neurogenic Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (NTOS), Double Crush Syndrome.
	- Tests & Measures Not Used: Thoracic ROM Assessment, Thoracic Outlet Test, Thoracic Manual Therapy, Cranial Nerve Exam.

	--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

	III) Cervical Radiculopathy

	Tests and Measures

	- T&M6. ULTT (Upper Limb Tension Testing): This test assesses nerve root involvement, which is key in diagnosing cervical radiculopathy.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: The ULTT is designed to evaluate the sensitivity of the peripheral nerves and nerve roots to tension or stretch. It helps identify nerve root compression or irritation, which are common in cervical radiculopathy.
	  - Procedure: The test involves placing the upper limb in positions that progressively increase tension on the nerve roots, such as shoulder abduction, elbow extension, wrist extension, and cervical lateral flexion away from the tested side.
	  - Clinical Importance: A positive ULTT indicates heightened nerve sensitivity or compression, suggesting cervical radiculopathy. It helps differentiate between radicular and non-radicular sources of arm pain.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Reproduction of Symptoms: Pain, tingling, or numbness in the arm that replicates the patient's symptoms.
	  - Limited Range of Motion: Inability to fully extend the elbow or wrist due to pain or discomfort.
	  - Increased Symptoms with Contralateral Cervical Flexion: Worsening of symptoms when the neck is bent away from the tested side.
	  - Relief with Ipsilateral Cervical Flexion: Reduction of symptoms when the neck is bent towards the tested side.

	- T&M10a. Spurling’s Test: This provocative test can help identify cervical radiculopathy by reproducing symptoms.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: Spurling’s test is used to provoke symptoms of nerve root compression by narrowing the intervertebral foramina, where nerve roots exit the spine.
	  - Procedure: The test involves extending and rotating the patient's neck to the affected side, followed by applying a downward compressive force on the head.
	  - Clinical Importance: A positive Spurling’s test suggests cervical radiculopathy due to foraminal narrowing or disc herniation, helping to confirm the diagnosis.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Reproduction of Radicular Pain: Pain radiating down the arm that mimics the patient's usual symptoms.
	  - Increased Neck Pain: Localized pain in the neck that may accompany radicular symptoms.
	  - Neurological Symptoms: Tingling, numbness, or weakness in the arm or hand.

	- T&M10b. Shoulder Abduction Test: This test can relieve symptoms, indicating nerve root involvement.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: The shoulder abduction test is used to relieve tension on the brachial plexus and cervical nerve roots, which can reduce radicular symptoms.
	  - Procedure: The patient is asked to place their hand on top of their head, which can alleviate symptoms by reducing tension on the affected nerve root.
	  - Clinical Importance: Relief of symptoms during this test suggests cervical radiculopathy, as it indicates that nerve root compression is relieved when tension is reduced.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Symptom Relief: Reduction or elimination of arm pain or tingling when the hand is placed on the head.
	  - Decreased Neurological Symptoms: Reduction in numbness or weakness in the arm.
	  - Improved Range of Motion: Increased ability to move the neck or arm without discomfort.

	Conditions and Tests & Measures Not Used

	- Conditions Not Addressed: Cervical Artery Disease (CAD), Cervical Myelopathy, Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV), Cervicogenic Pain/Headache Syndrome, Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (TOS), Double Crush Syndrome.
	- Tests & Measures Not Used: Vertebral Artery Testing, Cranial Nerve Exam, Thoracic ROM Assessment, Thoracic Outlet Test, Thoracic Manual Therapy.

	These tests and measures help clinicians accurately diagnose cervical radiculopathy by identifying nerve root involvement and differentiating it from other potential causes of neck and arm pain.

	--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

	IV) Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV)

	Tests and Measures

	- T&M7. Cranial Nerve Exam: While not specific to BPPV, a cranial nerve exam helps rule out other neurological causes of vertigo.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: The cranial nerve exam is a comprehensive assessment of the twelve cranial nerves, which control a variety of functions including sensory and motor activities. Although BPPV is primarily a vestibular disorder, a cranial nerve exam is crucial in ruling out central nervous system causes of vertigo, such as brainstem or cerebellar lesions, which could mimic BPPV symptoms.
	  - Procedure: The exam involves testing each cranial nerve's function, including eye movements, facial muscle strength, hearing, and balance. This helps identify any deficits that could indicate a central lesion rather than a peripheral vestibular issue like BPPV.
	  - Clinical Importance: Differentiating between peripheral (BPPV) and central causes of vertigo is critical for appropriate management. Central causes often require different interventions and may indicate more serious underlying conditions.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Abnormal Eye Movements: Nystagmus or other involuntary eye movements could indicate central nervous system involvement.
	  - Hearing Loss: Unilateral hearing loss may suggest a central cause if not associated with BPPV.
	  - Facial Weakness: Weakness or asymmetry in facial muscles could indicate a cranial nerve lesion.
	  - Dysphagia or Dysarthria: Difficulty swallowing or speaking may point to brainstem involvement.
	  - Ataxia: Lack of coordination or balance issues could suggest cerebellar involvement.

	Conditions and Tests & Measures Not Used

	- Conditions Not Addressed: Cervical Artery Disease (CAD), Cervical Myelopathy, Cervical Radiculopathy, Cervicogenic Pain/Headache Syndrome, Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (TOS), Double Crush Syndrome.
	- Tests & Measures Not Used: Vertebral Artery Testing, Upper Limb Tension Testing (ULTT), Spurling’s Test, Shoulder Abduction Test, Thoracic ROM Assessment, Thoracic Outlet Test, Thoracic Manual Therapy.

	The cranial nerve exam is a valuable tool in the differential diagnosis of vertigo, ensuring that clinicians can distinguish between peripheral vestibular disorders like BPPV and more serious central nervous system pathologies. This differentiation is essential for directing appropriate treatment and management strategies.

	--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

	V) Cervicogenic Pain/Headache Syndrome (With signs of hypermobility/instability)

	Tests and Measures

	- T&M1b. Sharp’s Purser Test: This test assesses for atlantoaxial instability, which is relevant for cervicogenic pain with hypermobility.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: The Sharp’s Purser Test is used to evaluate the stability of the atlantoaxial joint, particularly the integrity of the transverse ligament. Instability in this area can lead to excessive movement between the atlas (C1) and axis (C2), contributing to cervicogenic headaches and neck pain.
	  - Procedure: The patient is seated, and the clinician stabilizes the C2 vertebra with one hand while applying a posteriorly directed force to the forehead with the other hand. A positive test is indicated by a reduction in symptoms or a palpable clunk, suggesting that the atlas is reducing on the axis.
	  - Clinical Importance: Identifying atlantoaxial instability is crucial for preventing further complications, such as spinal cord compression. It guides treatment decisions, such as avoiding manipulative therapy in patients with confirmed instability.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Reduction of Symptoms: Relief of headache or neck pain when the test is performed.
	  - Palpable Clunk: A noticeable clunk during the test, indicating movement of the atlas on the axis.
	  - Feeling of Instability: The patient may report a sensation of instability or shifting in the neck.
	  - Neurological Symptoms: Presence of symptoms such as dizziness or tingling that improve with the test.

	- T&M1c. Alar Ligament Stress Test: This test evaluates the integrity of the alar ligaments, which can indicate instability.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: The Alar Ligament Stress Test assesses the stability of the alar ligaments, which connect the dens of the axis (C2) to the occipital bone. These ligaments limit rotation and side bending of the head, and their compromise can lead to instability and cervicogenic headaches.
	  - Procedure: The clinician stabilizes the axis and attempts to side-bend or rotate the head. A positive test occurs if there is excessive movement or a delay in the movement of the axis, indicating laxity or rupture of the alar ligaments.
	  - Clinical Importance: Detecting alar ligament instability is important for determining safe treatment options and preventing further injury. It may necessitate modifications in physical therapy approaches to avoid exacerbating the instability.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Excessive Movement: Greater than expected movement during the test, suggesting ligament laxity.
	  - Delayed Movement of C2: A delay in the movement of the axis when the head is side-bent or rotated.
	  - Reproduction of Symptoms: Provocation of headache or neck pain during the test.
	  - Neurological Symptoms: Symptoms such as dizziness or visual disturbances that occur during the test.

	Conditions and Tests & Measures Not Used

	- Conditions Not Addressed: Cervical Artery Disease (CAD), Cervical Myelopathy, Cervical Radiculopathy, Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV), Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (TOS), Double Crush Syndrome.
	- Tests & Measures Not Used: Vertebral Artery Testing, Upper Limb Tension Testing (ULTT), Spurling’s Test, Shoulder Abduction Test, Cranial Nerve Exam, Thoracic ROM Assessment, Thoracic Outlet Test, Thoracic Manual Therapy.

	These tests and measures are critical in identifying cervicogenic pain with signs of hypermobility or instability. They help clinicians determine the presence of ligamentous instability and guide appropriate management strategies to prevent further complications and provide effective treatment.

	--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

	VI) Cervicogenic Pain/Headache Syndrome (Without signs of hypermobility/instability)

	Tests and Measures

	- T&M8. Palpation: This test identifies muscle tension and trigger points contributing to headaches.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: Palpation is used to assess the soft tissues of the cervical region, including muscles, tendons, and ligaments, to identify areas of tenderness, tension, or trigger points that may contribute to cervicogenic headaches.
	  - Procedure: The clinician uses their hands to gently palpate the cervical musculature, noting any areas of increased tension or tenderness. Special attention is given to the suboccipital region, upper trapezius, and levator scapulae muscles.
	  - Clinical Importance: Identifying trigger points or areas of muscle tension can help determine the source of cervicogenic headaches. This information guides treatment strategies such as manual therapy, stretching, or strengthening exercises to alleviate pain.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Tenderness: Pain upon palpation of specific muscles or regions.
	  - Referred Pain: Pain that radiates to the head or other areas when a trigger point is palpated.
	  - Muscle Tightness: Increased muscle tone or resistance to palpation.
	  - Limited Range of Motion: Restricted movement due to muscle tension.

	- T&M11. Cervical Manual Therapy: Techniques like cervical distraction or PA glides can help relieve cervicogenic pain.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: Cervical manual therapy aims to improve joint mobility, reduce muscle tension, and alleviate pain through hands-on techniques. It is particularly beneficial for patients with cervicogenic headaches without instability.
	  - Procedure: Techniques include cervical distraction, which gently separates the vertebrae to relieve pressure, and posterior-anterior (PA) glides, which mobilize specific segments of the cervical spine to improve movement and reduce pain.
	  - Clinical Importance: Manual therapy can effectively reduce pain and improve function in patients with cervicogenic headaches by addressing mechanical dysfunctions in the cervical spine. It is a key component of a comprehensive treatment plan.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Pain Relief: Reduction in headache or neck pain following manual therapy.
	  - Increased Range of Motion: Improved cervical mobility after treatment.
	  - Decreased Muscle Tension: Reduction in muscle tightness and tenderness.
	  - Improved Function: Enhanced ability to perform daily activities without pain.

	Conditions and Tests & Measures Not Used

	- Conditions Not Addressed: Cervical Artery Disease (CAD), Cervical Myelopathy, Cervical Radiculopathy, Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV), Cervicogenic Pain/Headache Syndrome (With signs of hypermobility/instability), Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (TOS), Double Crush Syndrome.
	- Tests & Measures Not Used: Vertebral Artery Testing, Sharp’s Purser Test, Alar Ligament Stress Test, Upper Limb Tension Testing (ULTT), Spurling’s Test, Shoulder Abduction Test, Cranial Nerve Exam, Thoracic ROM Assessment, Thoracic Outlet Test, Thoracic Manual Therapy.

	These tests and measures are essential for diagnosing and managing cervicogenic pain/headache syndrome without signs of hypermobility or instability. They help clinicians identify the musculoskeletal sources of pain and guide appropriate interventions to relieve symptoms and improve patient outcomes.

	--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

	Neck Pain Classifications

	NP1. Neck Pain with Mobility Deficits

	Tests and Measures

	- T&M4. Cervical ROM Assessment (AROM and PROM): This assessment identifies limitations in mobility.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: Cervical Range of Motion (ROM) assessment is crucial for evaluating the degree of movement available in the cervical spine. It helps identify any restrictions or asymmetries in movement that may contribute to neck pain.
	  - Procedure: Active Range of Motion (AROM) involves the patient moving their neck through different planes (flexion, extension, lateral flexion, and rotation) without assistance. Passive Range of Motion (PROM) is assessed by the clinician moving the patient's neck through these same planes to determine if there are differences in movement when the patient is relaxed.
	  - Clinical Importance: Identifying limitations in ROM can help pinpoint specific areas of dysfunction or stiffness in the cervical spine. This information is critical for developing a targeted treatment plan to improve mobility and reduce pain.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Restricted Movement: Limited ability to move the neck in one or more directions.
	  - Pain with Movement: Discomfort or pain during neck movements, indicating possible joint or soft tissue restrictions.
	  - Asymmetry: Unequal movement between the left and right sides of the neck.
	  - End-Range Pain: Pain occurring at the end of the available range of motion, suggesting joint or soft tissue involvement.

	- T&M11. Cervical Manual Therapy: Techniques to improve mobility.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: Cervical manual therapy involves hands-on techniques to improve joint mobility, reduce muscle tension, and alleviate pain in the cervical spine.
	  - Procedure: Techniques may include cervical distraction, posterior-anterior (PA) glides, and side glides. These techniques are applied to specific segments of the cervical spine to enhance movement and relieve pain.
	  - Clinical Importance: Manual therapy can effectively address mechanical restrictions in the cervical spine, improving overall mobility and reducing pain. It is an integral part of a comprehensive treatment plan for patients with neck pain and mobility deficits.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Increased Range of Motion: Improvement in cervical mobility following manual therapy.
	  - Pain Relief: Reduction in neck pain after treatment.
	  - Decreased Muscle Tension: Reduction in muscle tightness and tenderness.
	  - Improved Function: Enhanced ability to perform daily activities without pain.

	Conditions and Tests & Measures Not Used

	- Conditions Not Addressed: Cervical Artery Disease (CAD), Cervical Myelopathy, Cervical Radiculopathy, Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV), Cervicogenic Pain/Headache Syndrome (With and Without signs of hypermobility/instability), Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (TOS), Double Crush Syndrome.
	- Tests & Measures Not Used: Vertebral Artery Testing, Sharp’s Purser Test, Alar Ligament Stress Test, Upper Limb Tension Testing (ULTT), Spurling’s Test, Shoulder Abduction Test, Cranial Nerve Exam, Thoracic Outlet Test, Thoracic Manual Therapy.

	These tests and measures are essential for diagnosing and managing neck pain with mobility deficits. They help clinicians identify specific areas of restriction in the cervical spine and guide appropriate interventions to improve mobility and reduce pain.

	--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

	NP2. Neck Pain with Movement Coordination Impairments

	Tests and Measures

	- T&M9. Muscle Performance: This assessment evaluates coordination and muscle function, which are critical in identifying movement coordination impairments associated with neck pain.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: Muscle performance testing is designed to assess the strength, endurance, and coordination of the neck and upper shoulder girdle muscles. This helps identify deficits that may contribute to movement coordination impairments.
	  - Procedure: The clinician evaluates the patient's ability to perform specific movements or maintain postures that require coordinated muscle activity. This may include isometric holds, dynamic movements, and endurance tests for cervical and scapular muscles.
	  - Clinical Importance: Identifying muscle performance deficits is crucial for developing a targeted rehabilitation program. Improving muscle strength and coordination can enhance stability and function, reducing neck pain and preventing further injury.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Muscle Weakness: Inability to generate sufficient force during isometric or dynamic testing.
	  - Poor Coordination: Difficulty performing smooth and controlled movements, indicating neuromuscular control issues.
	  - Fatigue: Rapid onset of muscle fatigue during endurance testing, suggesting poor muscle endurance.
	  - Postural Instability: Inability to maintain proper posture during testing, indicating deficits in muscle control and coordination.
	  - Pain During Testing: Reproduction of neck pain during muscle performance tasks, suggesting involvement of specific muscle groups in the patient's symptoms.

	Conditions and Tests & Measures Not Used

	- Conditions Not Addressed: Cervical Artery Disease (CAD), Cervical Myelopathy, Cervical Radiculopathy, Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV), Cervicogenic Pain/Headache Syndrome (With and Without signs of hypermobility/instability), Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (TOS), Double Crush Syndrome.
	- Tests & Measures Not Used: Vertebral Artery Testing, Sharp’s Purser Test, Alar Ligament Stress Test, Upper Limb Tension Testing (ULTT), Spurling’s Test, Shoulder Abduction Test, Cranial Nerve Exam, Cervical ROM Assessment, Thoracic ROM Assessment, Thoracic Outlet Test, Thoracic Manual Therapy.

	Muscle performance testing is essential for diagnosing and managing neck pain with movement coordination impairments. It helps clinicians identify specific muscle deficits and guide interventions to improve neuromuscular control, enhance stability, and reduce pain.

	--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

	NP3. Neck Pain with Cervicogenic Headache

	Tests and Measures

	- T&M8. Palpation: This test identifies muscle and joint dysfunction contributing to headaches.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: Palpation is a hands-on assessment technique used to detect areas of tenderness, muscle tension, and joint dysfunction in the cervical region that may contribute to cervicogenic headaches. These headaches are often caused by musculoskeletal issues in the neck, such as tight muscles or joint restrictions.
	  - Procedure: The clinician uses their fingers to gently palpate the cervical spine, focusing on the suboccipital region, upper trapezius, and other neck muscles. The clinician assesses for trigger points, areas of increased tension, or joint stiffness that could be linked to headache symptoms.
	  - Clinical Importance: Identifying specific musculoskeletal contributors to cervicogenic headaches allows for targeted treatment interventions, such as manual therapy or specific exercises, to alleviate pain and improve function.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Tenderness: Pain upon palpation of specific muscles or joints in the cervical region.
	  - Referred Pain: Pain that radiates to the head or other areas when a trigger point is palpated.
	  - Muscle Tightness: Increased muscle tone or resistance to palpation, indicating tension.
	  - Joint Stiffness: Reduced mobility or stiffness in the cervical joints upon palpation.

	Conditions and Tests & Measures Not Used

	- Conditions Not Addressed: Cervical Artery Disease (CAD), Cervical Myelopathy, Cervical Radiculopathy, Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV), Cervicogenic Pain/Headache Syndrome (With signs of hypermobility/instability), Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (TOS), Double Crush Syndrome.
	- Tests & Measures Not Used: Vertebral Artery Testing, Sharp’s Purser Test, Alar Ligament Stress Test, Upper Limb Tension Testing (ULTT), Spurling’s Test, Shoulder Abduction Test, Cranial Nerve Exam, Cervical ROM Assessment, Thoracic ROM Assessment, Thoracic Outlet Test, Thoracic Manual Therapy.

	Palpation is a critical tool in diagnosing and managing cervicogenic headaches. It helps clinicians identify specific musculoskeletal factors contributing to headache symptoms, guiding effective treatment strategies to alleviate pain and improve patient outcomes.

	--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

	NP4. Neck Pain with Radiating Pain

	Tests and Measures

	- T&M6. ULTT (Upper Limb Tension Testing): This test identifies nerve root involvement, which is crucial for diagnosing cervical radiculopathy, a common cause of neck pain with radiating symptoms.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: The Upper Limb Tension Test (ULTT) is used to assess the sensitivity of the peripheral nerves and nerve roots to tension or stretch. It helps identify nerve root compression or irritation, which can cause radiating pain from the neck into the arm.
	  - Procedure: The test involves positioning the patient's arm in a sequence of movements that progressively increase tension on the nerve roots, such as shoulder abduction, elbow extension, wrist extension, and cervical lateral flexion away from the tested side.
	  - Clinical Importance: A positive ULTT suggests heightened nerve sensitivity or compression, indicating cervical radiculopathy. This test helps differentiate between radicular and non-radicular sources of arm pain.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Reproduction of Symptoms: Pain, tingling, or numbness in the arm that replicates the patient's symptoms.
	  - Limited Range of Motion: Inability to fully extend the elbow or wrist due to pain or discomfort.
	  - Increased Symptoms with Contralateral Cervical Flexion: Worsening of symptoms when the neck is bent away from the tested side.
	  - Relief with Ipsilateral Cervical Flexion: Reduction of symptoms when the neck is bent towards the tested side.

	- T&M10a. Spurling’s Test: This provocative test is used for identifying cervical radiculopathy by reproducing symptoms of radiating pain.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: Spurling’s test is designed to provoke symptoms of nerve root compression by narrowing the intervertebral foramina, where nerve roots exit the spine.
	  - Procedure: The test involves extending and rotating the patient's neck to the affected side, followed by applying a downward compressive force on the head.
	  - Clinical Importance: A positive Spurling’s test suggests cervical radiculopathy due to foraminal narrowing or disc herniation, helping to confirm the diagnosis of neck pain with radiating symptoms.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Reproduction of Radicular Pain: Pain radiating down the arm that mimics the patient's usual symptoms.
	  - Increased Neck Pain: Localized pain in the neck that may accompany radicular symptoms.
	  - Neurological Symptoms: Tingling, numbness, or weakness in the arm or hand.

	Conditions and Tests & Measures Not Used

	- Conditions Not Addressed: Cervical Artery Disease (CAD), Cervical Myelopathy, Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV), Cervicogenic Pain/Headache Syndrome (With and Without signs of hypermobility/instability), Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (TOS), Double Crush Syndrome.
	- Tests & Measures Not Used: Vertebral Artery Testing, Sharp’s Purser Test, Alar Ligament Stress Test, Shoulder Abduction Test, Cranial Nerve Exam, Cervical ROM Assessment, Thoracic ROM Assessment, Thoracic Outlet Test, Thoracic Manual Therapy.

	These tests and measures are essential for diagnosing and managing neck pain with radiating pain. They help clinicians identify specific nerve root involvement and differentiate it from other potential causes of neck and arm pain, guiding effective treatment strategies.

	--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

	Thoracic Conditions

	TP1. Vascular Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (TOS) / Vascular TOS Compression

	Tests and Measures

	- T&M12. Thoracic Outlet Test: Tests like Roos or Adson’s can help identify vascular TOS.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (TOS) encompasses a group of disorders that occur when blood vessels or nerves in the thoracic outlet are compressed. Vascular TOS specifically refers to the compression of blood vessels, which can lead to compromised blood flow. Tests like Roos and Adson’s are designed to provoke symptoms by positioning the arms and neck in ways that may compress the thoracic outlet.
	  - Procedure:
	    - Roos Test: The patient is asked to abduct their arms to 90 degrees, externally rotate the shoulders, and flex the elbows to 90 degrees. They then open and close their hands slowly for three minutes. A positive test is indicated by the onset of symptoms such as pain, heaviness, or numbness.
	    - Adson’s Test: The patient is instructed to take a deep breath and hold it while extending the neck and turning the head toward the affected side. The clinician palpates the radial pulse. A positive test is indicated by a decrease or absence of the radial pulse or reproduction of symptoms.
	  - Clinical Importance: Identifying vascular TOS is crucial for preventing complications such as blood clots or aneurysms. These tests help differentiate between vascular and neurogenic TOS, guiding appropriate treatment strategies.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Decreased or Absent Pulse: A reduction or loss of the radial pulse during Adson’s test suggests vascular compression.
	  - Pain or Heaviness: Discomfort or a feeling of heaviness in the arm during Roos test indicates possible vascular involvement.
	  - Numbness or Tingling: Sensory changes in the arm or hand during testing suggest compromised blood flow.
	  - Color Changes: Pallor or cyanosis in the hand during testing may indicate vascular insufficiency.
	  - Swelling: Edema in the arm or hand, potentially due to venous compression.

	Conditions and Tests & Measures Not Used

	- Conditions Not Addressed: Cervical Artery Disease (CAD), Cervical Myelopathy, Cervical Radiculopathy, Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV), Cervicogenic Pain/Headache Syndrome (With and Without signs of hypermobility/instability), Neurogenic Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (NTOS), Double Crush Syndrome.
	- Tests & Measures Not Used: Vertebral Artery Testing, Sharp’s Purser Test, Alar Ligament Stress Test, Upper Limb Tension Testing (ULTT), Spurling’s Test, Shoulder Abduction Test, Cranial Nerve Exam, Cervical ROM Assessment, Thoracic ROM Assessment, Thoracic Manual Therapy.

	These tests are essential for diagnosing vascular TOS by identifying vascular compression in the thoracic outlet. They help clinicians differentiate between vascular and neurogenic causes of symptoms, ensuring accurate diagnosis and effective treatment.

	--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

	TP2. Neurogenic Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (NTOS)

	Tests and Measures

	- T&M12. Thoracic Outlet Test: Tests like Roos or Adson’s can help identify neurogenic TOS.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: Neurogenic Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (NTOS) is a condition where the brachial plexus nerves are compressed as they pass through the thoracic outlet, leading to neurological symptoms. Tests like Roos and Adson’s are designed to provoke symptoms by positioning the arms and neck in ways that may compress the neurovascular structures in the thoracic outlet.
	  - Procedure:
	    - Roos Test: The patient is asked to abduct their arms to 90 degrees, externally rotate the shoulders, and flex the elbows to 90 degrees. They then open and close their hands slowly for three minutes. A positive test is indicated by the onset of symptoms such as pain, numbness, or tingling.
	    - Adson’s Test: The patient is instructed to take a deep breath and hold it while extending the neck and turning the head toward the affected side. The clinician palpates the radial pulse. A positive test is indicated by a decrease or absence of the radial pulse or reproduction of symptoms.
	  - Clinical Importance: Identifying NTOS is crucial for preventing chronic pain and disability. These tests help differentiate between neurogenic and vascular TOS, guiding appropriate treatment strategies.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Numbness or Tingling: Sensory changes in the arm or hand during testing suggest nerve compression.
	  - Weakness: Reduced strength in the hand or arm, potentially due to nerve involvement.
	  - Pain: Discomfort or aching in the neck, shoulder, or arm during testing.
	  - Reproduction of Symptoms: Provocation of the patient's usual symptoms during the test.
	  - Decreased or Absent Pulse: Although more indicative of vascular TOS, a change in pulse during Adson’s test can suggest neurovascular involvement.

	Conditions and Tests & Measures Not Used

	- Conditions Not Addressed: Cervical Artery Disease (CAD), Cervical Myelopathy, Cervical Radiculopathy, Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV), Cervicogenic Pain/Headache Syndrome (With and Without signs of hypermobility/instability), Vascular Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (TOS), Double Crush Syndrome.
	- Tests & Measures Not Used: Vertebral Artery Testing, Sharp’s Purser Test, Alar Ligament Stress Test, Upper Limb Tension Testing (ULTT), Spurling’s Test, Shoulder Abduction Test, Cranial Nerve Exam, Cervical ROM Assessment, Thoracic ROM Assessment, Thoracic Manual Therapy.

	These tests are essential for diagnosing neurogenic TOS by identifying compression of the brachial plexus in the thoracic outlet. They help clinicians differentiate between neurogenic and vascular causes of symptoms, ensuring accurate diagnosis and effective treatment.

	--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

	TP3. Double Crush Syndrome

	Tests and Measures

	- T&M6. ULTT (Upper Limb Tension Testing): This test assesses nerve involvement, which can be part of double crush syndrome.

	  Rationale
	  - Purpose: Double Crush Syndrome refers to the phenomenon where a peripheral nerve is compressed at two or more locations along its path. The Upper Limb Tension Test (ULTT) is designed to assess the sensitivity of the peripheral nerves and nerve roots to tension or stretch, helping to identify areas of nerve compression.
	  - Procedure: The ULTT involves positioning the patient's arm in a sequence of movements that progressively increase tension on the nerve roots and peripheral nerves, such as shoulder abduction, elbow extension, wrist extension, and cervical lateral flexion away from the tested side.
	  - Clinical Importance: A positive ULTT suggests heightened nerve sensitivity or compression, which is indicative of nerve involvement. In the context of Double Crush Syndrome, it helps identify multiple sites of compression, such as at the cervical spine and a distal site like the carpal tunnel.

	  Signs & Symptoms Indicating a Positive Test
	  - Reproduction of Symptoms: Pain, tingling, or numbness in the arm that replicates the patient's symptoms.
	  - Limited Range of Motion: Inability to fully extend the elbow or wrist due to pain or discomfort.
	  - Increased Symptoms with Contralateral Cervical Flexion: Worsening of symptoms when the neck is bent away from the tested side.
	  - Relief with Ipsilateral Cervical Flexion: Reduction of symptoms when the neck is bent towards the tested side.

	Conditions and Tests & Measures Not Used

	- Conditions Not Addressed: Cervical Artery Disease (CAD), Cervical Myelopathy, Cervical Radiculopathy, Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV), Cervicogenic Pain/Headache Syndrome (With and Without signs of hypermobility/instability), Vascular Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (TOS), Neurogenic Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (NTOS).
	- Tests & Measures Not Used: Vertebral Artery Testing, Sharp’s Purser Test, Alar Ligament Stress Test, Spurling’s Test, Shoulder Abduction Test, Cranial Nerve Exam, Cervical ROM Assessment, Thoracic ROM Assessment, Thoracic Outlet Test, Thoracic Manual Therapy.

	The ULTT is a valuable tool for diagnosing Double Crush Syndrome by identifying nerve involvement and potential sites of compression. This helps clinicians develop comprehensive treatment plans to address multiple areas of nerve compression and alleviate symptoms.

	--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

	SUMMARY

	I) Cervical Artery Disease (CAD)
	- Tests and Measures:
	  - T&M1a. Vertebral Artery Testing: Specifically designed to assess the integrity of the vertebral arteries, crucial for identifying potential CAD.

	II) Cervical Myelopathy
	- Tests and Measures:
	  - T&M2. UQS (Upper Quarter Screen): Includes upper motor neuron testing, which is relevant for identifying cervical myelopathy.
	  - T&M4. Cervical ROM Assessment (AROM and PROM): Helps identify limitations or abnormalities in movement that might suggest myelopathy.
	  - T&M4a. AROM and T&M4b. PROM: Assess active and passive range of motion, which can indicate myelopathy if there are significant restrictions or neurological signs.
	  - T&M10. Special Tests: Specific tests like Hoffman's sign or Babinski can indicate upper motor neuron involvement.

	III) Cervical Radiculopathy
	- Tests and Measures:
	  - T&M6. ULTT (Upper Limb Tension Testing): Assesses nerve root involvement, which is key in diagnosing cervical radiculopathy.
	  - T&M10a. Spurling’s Test: Provocative test that can help identify cervical radiculopathy by reproducing symptoms.
	  - T&M10b. Shoulder Abduction Test: Can relieve symptoms, indicating nerve root involvement.

	IV) Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (BPPV)
	- Tests and Measures:
	  - T&M7. Cranial Nerve Exam: While not specific to BPPV, it helps rule out other causes of vertigo.

	V) Cervicogenic Pain/Headache Syndrome (With signs of hypermobility/instability)
	- Tests and Measures:
	  - T&M1b. Sharp’s Purser Test: Assesses for atlantoaxial instability, relevant for cervicogenic pain with hypermobility.
	  - T&M1c. Alar Ligament Stress Test: Evaluates the integrity of the alar ligaments, which can indicate instability.

	VI) Cervicogenic Pain/Headache Syndrome (Without signs of hypermobility/instability)
	- Tests and Measures:
	  - T&M8. Palpation: Identifies muscle tension and trigger points contributing to headaches.
	  - T&M11. Cervical Manual Therapy: Techniques like cervical distraction or PA glides can help relieve cervicogenic pain.

	Neck Pain Classifications

	1. NP1. Neck Pain with Mobility Deficits
	   - Tests and Measures:
	     - T&M4. Cervical ROM Assessment (AROM and PROM): Identifies limitations in mobility.
	     - T&M11. Cervical Manual Therapy: Techniques to improve mobility.

	2. NP2. Neck Pain with Movement Coordination Impairments
	   - Tests and Measures:
	     - T&M9. Muscle Performance: Assesses coordination and muscle function.

	3. NP3. Neck Pain with Cervicogenic Headache
	   - Tests and Measures:
	     - T&M8. Palpation: Identifies muscle and joint dysfunction contributing to headaches.

	4. NP4. Neck Pain with Radiating Pain
	   - Tests and Measures:
	     - T&M6. ULTT: Identifies nerve root involvement.
	     - T&M10a. Spurling’s Test: Provocative test for radiating pain.

	Thoracic Conditions

	1. TP1. Vascular TOS / Vascular TOS Compression
	   - Tests and Measures:
	     - T&M12. Thoracic Outlet Test: Tests like Roos or Adson’s can help identify vascular TOS.

	2. TP2. Neurogenic Thoracic Outlet Syndrome (NTOS)
	   - Tests and Measures:
	     - T&M12. Thoracic Outlet Test: Tests like Roos or Adson’s can help identify neurogenic TOS.

	3. TP3. Double Crush Syndrome
	   - Tests and Measures:
	     - T&M6. ULTT: Assesses nerve involvement, which can be part of double crush syndrome.

	Report of Conditions and Tests Not Used

	- BPPV: No specific positional tests like Dix-Hallpike were listed.
	- Cervical Artery Disease (CAD): Only vertebral artery testing is directly related.
	- Cervical Myelopathy: No specific gait analysis or detailed upper motor neuron tests were listed.
	- Cervicogenic Pain/Headache Syndrome (Without signs of hypermobility/instability): No specific tests for non-hypermobile cervicogenic pain were listed beyond palpation and manual therapy.

	This classification helps clinicians select appropriate tests to narrow down potential conditions based on patient symptoms and clinical presentation.

.Recommended Exercises for Treatment of the 4 Main Conditions
	PROMPT: Give me examples of real exercises that I can apply in a treatment for a patient with "___". Base your answer in the following information:

	1.Neck Pain With Mobility Deficits
		Based on the information provided for treating Neck Pain With Mobility Deficits, here are examples of real exercises that can be applied in treatment:

		1. Manipulation/Mobilization of C-Spine/T-Spine:
		   While these are typically performed by a therapist, some self-mobilization techniques can be taught:
		   - Self-cervical traction: Using a towel around the base of the skull, gently pull upward while in a seated position.
		   - Thoracic self-mobilization: Using a foam roller, lie supine and perform gentle thoracic extensions.
		   What manual techniques can be applied by the therapist?
		   		Γ These are presented in the document as "assessment/testing", but they can be transformed in "Mobilization and Manipulation" 
			   		('79 Mod6 Accessory Motion of the Cervical Spine.mp4')
					   	P-A glides/Spring testing​ 
						Sideglides
					Segmental Testing​ ('80 Mod6 Supine Cervical Passive Mobility.mp4')
					(See 'TrMT.Manual Therapy [MSK2_FinalExam_Repas]') ← Pots agafar algo d'ací per al torax
						O bé els tests que ens van recomanar en "Remediation Plan"
							-(See 'Remediation Plan MSK2 Cervical & Shoulder:: Cervical Skill List::Γ"6.15. Manual Therapy for the Cervical Spine" + "::40.Cervical Manual Therapy Techniques"')
								-Cervical PA glide: Prone and supine
						 		-Cervical Side Glide
						 		-Thoracic PA Glides: Unilateral and Central ← Γ"Thoracic PA Glides (Unilateral & Central) Assessment/Treatment"
		   	--------------------------------------------------------------
		   	Based on the information provided for treating Neck Pain With Mobility Deficits, here are examples of manual techniques that can be applied by the therapist:

			1. Cervical Spine Manipulation:
			   - High-velocity, low-amplitude (HVLA) thrust techniques to specific cervical segments
			   - These should be performed with caution and only by trained professionals

			2. Cervical Spine Mobilization:
			   - Grade I-IV joint mobilizations to the cervical spine
			   - These can include posterior-anterior (PA) glides, lateral glides, and rotational mobilizations

			3. Thoracic Spine Manipulation:
			   - HVLA thrust techniques to the upper and mid-thoracic spine
			   - This can help improve overall cervical mobility and reduce referred pain

			4. Soft Tissue Mobilization:
			   - Manual techniques to address muscle tension and trigger points in the cervical and upper thoracic regions
			   - This may include techniques like myofascial release, trigger point therapy, and cross-friction massage

			5. Manual Cervical Traction:
			   - Gentle axial traction applied manually by the therapist to decompress cervical joints

			6. Muscle Energy Techniques:
			   - Using the patient's own muscle contractions to improve joint mobility and muscle flexibility

			7. Passive Range of Motion (PROM):
			   - Therapist-guided movements of the cervical spine through available ranges to improve mobility

			8. Joint Mobilization with Movement:
			   - Combining joint mobilization techniques with active movements performed by the patient

			9. Cranio-cervical Flexion Mobilization:
			   - Specific mobilization techniques targeting the upper cervical spine to improve deep neck flexor activation

			10. Scapulothoracic Mobilization:
			    - Manual techniques to improve mobility of the scapula and its relationship to the thoracic spine, which can indirectly affect cervical mobility

			Remember that these manual techniques should only be performed by qualified healthcare professionals with specific training in cervical spine manipulation and mobilization. The choice of technique and its application should be based on a thorough assessment of the patient's condition, considering any contraindications or precautions.

		2. ROM/Coordination/Strengthening/Endurance/Stretching Exercises:

		   a) Range of Motion (ROM) Exercises:
		   - Chin tucks: Gently tuck chin towards chest, then return to neutral.
		   - Cervical rotations: Slowly turn head side to side within a comfortable range.
		   - Cervical side bending: Tilt head towards each shoulder alternately.

		   b) Coordination Exercises:
		   - Head-eye coordination: Focus on a target, move only the eyes to look at it, then move the head to follow.
		   - Wall angel slides: Stand against a wall, perform arm raises while maintaining contact, coordinating with neck movements.

		   c) Strengthening Exercises:
		   - Isometric neck strengthening: Apply gentle pressure with hand against forehead, back of head, and sides, holding for 5-10 seconds without moving the head.
		   - Deep neck flexor activation: Lie supine, perform a gentle chin tuck and slightly lift head off the surface, holding for 5-10 seconds.

		   d) Endurance Exercises:
		   - Low-load cervical retraction: Perform gentle chin tucks, holding for 10 seconds, repeat 10-15 times.
		   - Prone cervical extension holds: Lie prone, lift head slightly off the surface, hold for 10-20 seconds.

		   e) Stretching Exercises:
		   - Upper trapezius stretch: Gently tilt head to one side, bringing ear towards shoulder, hold for 15-30 seconds.
		   - Levator scapulae stretch: Look down and diagonally to opposite side, gently pull head with hand, hold for 15-30 seconds.

		3. General Fitness Training:
		   - Brisk walking: Start with 10-15 minutes and gradually increase duration.
		   - Stationary cycling: Using an upright bike to maintain cardiovascular fitness without straining the neck.
		   - Swimming: Gentle swimming strokes, avoiding butterfly or aggressive head movements.

		4. Advice to Stay Active:
		   While not an exercise per se, educate the patient on:
		   - Maintaining daily activities within pain limits.
		   - Proper ergonomics for work and home environments.
		   - The importance of regular movement and avoiding prolonged static postures.

		Additional beneficial exercises:

		5. Postural Correction:
		   - Brugger's relief position: Sit with arms relaxed at sides, palms forward, chin tucked.
		   - Shoulder blade squeezes: Gently squeeze shoulder blades together while maintaining proper posture.

		6. Proprioception Training:
		   - Head repositioning accuracy: Close eyes, move head to a position, then try to return to neutral.
		   - Laser pointer tracking: Wear a laser pointer on head, trace patterns on wall while maintaining posture.

		Remember to tailor these exercises to the individual patient's needs, symptoms, and tolerance. Start with gentle, pain-free movements and progress gradually. Always instruct patients to stop if they experience increased pain or neurological symptoms. The focus should be on improving mobility, strengthening the neck and supporting muscles, and encouraging overall activity to promote recovery and prevent further mobility deficits.

	2.Neck Pain With Movement Coordination Impairment - Whiplash Associated Disorders (WAD)
		1. Coordination Exercises:
		   - Chin tucks with rotation: Perform a gentle chin tuck, then slowly rotate the head to each side within a comfortable range.
		   - Eye-head coordination: Focus on a target, move only the eyes to look at it, then move the head to follow.
		   - Wall angel slides: Stand with back against a wall, perform arm raises while maintaining contact with the wall, focusing on coordinated movement of the neck and shoulders.

		2. Strengthening Exercises:
		   - Isometric neck strengthening: Apply gentle pressure with hand against forehead, back of head, and sides, holding for 5-10 seconds without moving the head.
		   - Deep neck flexor activation: Lie supine, perform a gentle chin tuck and slightly lift head off the surface, holding for 5-10 seconds.
		   - Scapular retraction: Squeeze shoulder blades together, holding for 5-10 seconds.

		3. Endurance Exercises:
		   - Low-load cervical retraction: Perform gentle chin tucks, holding for 10 seconds, repeat 10-15 times.
		   - Prone cervical extension holds: Lie prone, lift head slightly off the surface, hold for 10-20 seconds.
		   - Wall slide holds: Perform wall angel slides, holding the top position for 10-20 seconds.

		4. Stretching Exercises:
		   - Upper trapezius stretch: Gently tilt head to one side, bringing ear towards shoulder, hold for 15-30 seconds.
		   - Levator scapulae stretch: Look down and diagonally to opposite side, gently pull head with hand, hold for 15-30 seconds.
		   - Pectoralis stretch: Stand in a doorway with arms on the frame, lean forward to feel a stretch in the chest muscles.

		5. Cervical Traction/Distraction:
		   - Manual cervical traction: A therapist gently applies axial traction to the neck.
		   - Home cervical traction device: Patient uses an over-the-door traction unit as instructed by the therapist.

		6. Education on Good Prognosis/Advice to Stay Active:
		   - Provide information on the generally good prognosis for WAD and the importance of staying active.
		   - Teach proper posture and ergonomics for daily activities.
		   - Encourage gradual return to normal activities as tolerated.

		Additional exercises that may be beneficial:

		7. Proprioception Training:
		   - Head repositioning accuracy: Close eyes, move head to a position, then try to return to neutral.
		   - Laser pointer tracking: Wear a laser pointer on head, trace patterns on wall while maintaining posture.

		8. Balance and Stability Exercises:
		   - Single-leg stance with head movements: Stand on one leg while performing gentle head rotations.
		   - Tandem stance with arm movements: Stand with one foot directly in front of the other, perform arm raises.

		9. Functional Movement Patterns:
		   - Controlled cervical rotations during walking: Practice turning head to look side to side while walking.
		   - Simulated work or daily living tasks: Perform common activities with focus on proper neck positioning and movement.

		Remember to tailor these exercises to the individual patient's needs, symptoms, and tolerance. Start with gentle, pain-free movements and progress gradually. Always instruct patients to stop if they experience increased pain or neurological symptoms. The focus should be on improving movement coordination, strengthening the neck and shoulder muscles, and gradually increasing the patient's confidence in moving their neck during daily activities.

		Emphasize the importance of staying active and maintaining a positive outlook on recovery. Educate the patient about the nature of WAD and how active participation in their rehabilitation can lead to better outcomes.

	3b.Craniovertebral Ligament Injury
		Based on the information provided and the specific case of a patient with Craniovertebral Ligament Injury, it's important to note that this condition is considered serious and potentially dangerous. The primary interventions focus on stabilization and referral to a specialist, with surgery being a likely outcome. However, while awaiting specialist care, some conservative measures can be taken under close medical supervision. Here are some examples of exercises and interventions that might be considered:

		1. Isometric Neck Strengthening:
		   - Gentle isometric contractions in neutral position
		   - Patient applies light pressure with hand against forehead, back of head, and sides of head, holding for 5-10 seconds without moving the head

		   - Teaching proper sitting and standing posture to minimize stress on the upper cervical spine
		   - Ergonomic adjustments for work and home environments

		4. Gentle Range of Motion Exercises:
		   - Very gentle, controlled nodding movements (flexion/extension) within a pain-free range
		   - Slight rotations, staying well within comfort zone

		5. Deep Neck Flexor Activation:
		   - Supine chin tucks: Lying on back, gently tucking chin towards chest without lifting head

		6. Scapular Stabilization:
		   - Shoulder blade squeezes: Gently squeezing shoulder blades together while maintaining proper posture

		7. Breathing Exercises:
		   - Diaphragmatic breathing to promote relaxation and reduce tension in neck muscles

		8. Soft Tissue Work:
		   - Gentle self-massage of upper trapezius and suboccipital muscles to reduce tension

		9. Use of Supportive Devices:
		   - Proper use of a soft cervical collar if prescribed by a physician
		   - Instruction on using supportive pillows for sleeping

		10. Education on Activity Modification:
		    - Teaching patient how to avoid activities that may exacerbate symptoms or put stress on the upper cervical spine

		It's crucial to emphasize that these exercises should only be performed under the direct supervision of a healthcare professional specializing in upper cervical injuries. The primary focus should be on stabilization and preventing further injury while awaiting specialist care. Any exercise program should be extremely conservative and tailored to the individual patient's condition.

		Always remind the patient to stop any exercise immediately if they experience increased pain, dizziness, visual disturbances, or any neurological symptoms. The overarching goal is to maintain the patient's safety while awaiting definitive treatment, which is likely to be surgical intervention.

	3c.Cervical Strain/Sprain
		Based on the provided information about intervention strategies for patients recovering from post-traumatic cervical injury (cervical strain/sprain), here are examples of real exercises that can be applied in treatment:

		1. R.I.C.E/P.R.I.C.E/P.O.L.I.C.E:
		   - Protection: Instruct the patient to avoid aggravating activities and maintain proper posture.
		   - Optimal Loading: Gradually introduce gentle neck movements within pain-free ranges.
		   - Ice: Apply ice packs to the affected area for 15-20 minutes every 2-3 hours.
		   - Compression: Use a soft cervical collar if prescribed by a physician.
		   - Elevation: Teach proper sleeping positions using supportive pillows.

		2. Aerobic activity as tolerated:
		   - Walking: Start with short, gentle walks and gradually increase duration.
		   - Stationary cycling: Using an upright bike to maintain cardiovascular fitness without straining the neck.

		3. ROM in pain-free ranges:
		   - Gentle neck rotations: Slowly turning head side to side within comfortable limits.
		   - Chin tucks: Gently tucking chin towards chest, holding for 5-10 seconds.
		   - Side bending: Carefully tilting head towards each shoulder.

		4. Joint mobilization as indicated:
		   - Gentle grade I-II mobilizations to the cervical spine for pain relief (performed by a therapist).

		5. Trial of cervical traction:
		   - Manual cervical traction: Therapist gently applies axial traction to the neck.
		   - Home cervical traction device: Patient uses a over-the-door traction unit as instructed.

		6. Gentle isometrics of cervical/postural/scapular muscles:
		   - Isometric neck flexion: Patient presses forehead into hand, holding for 5-10 seconds.
		   - Isometric neck extension: Pressing back of head into hand or surface.
		   - Shoulder blade squeezes: Gently squeezing shoulder blades together, holding for 5-10 seconds.

		7. Advanced cervical stabilization:
		   - Deep neck flexor activation: Performing chin tucks while lying supine, progressing to slight head lifts.
		   - Wall slides: Standing against a wall, sliding arms up and down while maintaining proper posture.

		8. Proprioceptive training:
		   - Laser tracking: Using a laser pointer attached to a headband, patient traces patterns on a wall.
		   - Head repositioning: Patient closes eyes, moves head slightly, then returns to neutral position.

		9. Progression through rehabilitation phases:

		   Phase 1 (Acute):
		   - Gentle PROM exercises within pain-free ranges.
		   - Soft tissue massage to reduce muscle guarding.

		   Phase 2 (Subacute):
		   - AROM exercises: Neck rotations, flexion/extension, and side bending.
		   - Light resistance exercises using therapy bands.

		   Phase 3 (Progressing/Chronic):
		   - Dynamic stability exercises: Ball rolls on wall with head, progressing to unstable surfaces.
		   - Functional movement patterns: Mimicking work or sport-specific movements at low intensity.

		   Phase 4 (High-level Function):
		   - Sport-specific drills: For athletes, incorporating neck movements into sport-specific activities.
		   - Work simulation: Replicating job tasks with proper form and gradually increasing intensity.

		Remember to tailor these exercises to the individual patient's needs, symptoms, and tolerance. Start with gentle, pain-free movements and progress gradually. Always instruct patients to stop if they experience increased pain or neurological symptoms. The treatment should follow the progression through the phases as the patient improves, with careful consideration of their specific condition and response to interventions.
